\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\varphi_{(0)}(\bm{q}_{1})\varphi_{(0)}(\bm{q}_{2})\varphi_{(0)}(\bm{q}_{3})\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\frac{\re\psi_{3}(\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2},\bm{q}_{3})}{\prod_{i=1}^{3}\re\psi_{2}(q_{i})},

(100)

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\varphi_{(0)}(\bm{q}_{1})\varphi_{(0)}(\bm{q}_{2})\varphi_{(0)}(\bm{q}_{3})\varphi_{(0)}(\bm{q}_{4})\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}\Big[\frac{\re\psi_{4}(\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2},\bm{q}_{3},\bm{q}_{4})}{\prod_{i=1}^{4}\re\psi_{2}(q_{i})}

\displaystyle\qquad\hskip-85.35826pt-\Big(\frac{\re\psi_{3}(\bm{q}_{1},\bm{q}_{2},\bm{q}_{12})\re\psi_{3}(-\bm{q}_{12},\bm{q}_{3},\bm{q}_{4})}{\re\psi_{2}(q_{12})\prod_{i=1}^{4}\re\psi_{2}(q_{i})}+(2\leftrightarrow 3)+(2\leftrightarrow 4)\Big)\Big]

(101)

where
\bm{q}_{ij}=\bm{q}_{i}+\bm{q}_{j}
.

Working in (A)dS units, the analytic continuation between CFT correlators and wavefunction coefficients is given by (
2.3.2
). Combining this with the relations (
2.4
) between wavefunction coefficients and dS correlators, we obtain the holographic formulae

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\varphi_{(0)}(\bm{q})\varphi_{(0)}(-\bm{q})\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{\re[(-i)^{d}\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}(\bm{q})\mathcal{O}(-\bm{q})\rangle\!\rangle]},

(102)

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\varphi_{(0)}(\bm{q}_{1})\varphi_{(0)}(\bm{q}_{2})\varphi_{(0)}(\bm{q}_{3})\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\frac{\re[(-i)^{d}\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}(\bm{q}_{1})\mathcal{O}(\bm{q}_{2})\mathcal{O}(\bm{q}_{3})\rangle\!\rangle]}{\prod_{i=1}^{3}\re[(-i)^{d}\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}(\bm{q}_{i})\mathcal{O}(-\bm{q}_{i})\rangle\!\rangle]},

(103)

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\varphi_{(0)}(\bm{q}_{1})\varphi_{(0)}(\bm{q}_{2})\varphi_{(0)}(\bm{q}_{3})\varphi_{(0)}(\bm{q}_{4})\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}\Big[\frac{\re[(-i)^{d}\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}(\bm{q}_{1})\mathcal{O}(\bm{q}_{2})\mathcal{O}(\bm{q}_{3})\mathcal{O}(\bm{q}_{4})\rangle\!\rangle]}{\prod_{i=1}^{4}\re[(-i)^{d}\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}(\bm{q}_{i})\mathcal{O}(-\bm{q}_{i})\rangle\!\rangle]}

\displaystyle\hskip-170.71652pt-\Big(\frac{\re[(-i)^{d}\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}(\bm{q}_{1})\mathcal{O}(\bm{q}_{2})\mathcal{O}(\bm{q}_{12})\rangle\!\rangle]\re[(-i)^{d}\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}(-\bm{q}_{12})\mathcal{O}(\bm{q}_{3})\mathcal{O}(\bm{q}_{4})\rangle\!\rangle]}{\re[(-i)^{d}\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}(\bm{q}_{12})\mathcal{O}(-\bm{q}_{12})\rangle\!\rangle]\prod_{i=1}^{4}\re[(-i)^{d}\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}(\bm{q}_{i})\mathcal{O}(-\bm{q}_{i})\rangle\!\rangle]}+(2\leftrightarrow 3)+(2\leftrightarrow 4)\Big)\Big]

(104)

where the real parts of all correlators on the right-hand sides are taken after continuing

\ell_{P}^{(AdS)}=-i\ell_{P}^{(dS)},\qquad q_{AdS}=iq_{dS}.

(105)

While our discussion has focused on a scalar field of a single type, the generalisation to multiple interacting scalar fields is straightforward: the continuations of propagators and vertices is the same and one arrives at the formulae above where the correlators on both sides are generalised so that each momentum is associated with a specific operator.

Finally, let us discuss the domain of validity of the various results above.
While the relation (
2.3.2
) between CFT correlators and wavefunction coefficients holds for any diagram, and hence to all orders in perturbation theory, the relations (
99
)-(
2.4
) between wavefunction coefficients and dS correlators holds only to leading order in
\ell_{P}^{(dS)}/L_{dS}
.
(Recall the dS action (
50
) carries an overall factor of
(L_{dS}/\ell_{P}^{(dS)})^{d-1}
.)
The holographic formula (
102
)-(
104
) are then valid to leading order in the large-
N
expansion of the dual CFT.

While here we have been discussing a fixed gravitational background for simplicity, formulae analogous to (
102
)-(
104
) have been derived for fully dynamical backgrounds at up to 3-points
McFadden:2009fg
;
McFadden:2010vh
;
McFadden:2011kk
. In this more general setting,
when the dual CFT is strongly interacting, the bulk gravity
theory is weakly coupled (
i.e.,
Einstein gravity holds and higher-curvature corrections are suppressed). Leading order in
\ell_{P}^{((A)dS)}/L_{(A)dS}
is then equivalent to tree level in the bulk loop expansion. However, the holographic duality is also expected to hold in the opposite regime where the CFT is weakly interacting (though still at large
N
) and the bulk (super)gravity approximation breaks down. Holographic formulae analogous to (
102
)-(
104
) should still be applicable in this limit, though on the bulk side leading order in
\ell_{P}^{((A)dS)}/L_{(A)dS}
now corresponds to an expansion in string loops.

3
Schwinger-Keldysh approach

The Schwinger-Keldysh or in-in formalism provides an alternative approach for computing cosmological correlators.
To arrive at this formalism, we first rewrite the wavefunction of the universe and its complex conjugate as

\Psi[\varphi_{(0)}]=\int\mathcal{D}\varphi_{+}e^{iS_{+}[\varphi_{+}]},\qquad\Psi^{*}[\varphi_{(0)}]=\int\mathcal{D}\varphi_{-}e^{-iS_{-}[\varphi_{-}]},

(106)

where
\varphi_{+}(\tau,\bm{x})
and
\varphi_{-}(\tau,\bm{x})
are regarded as independent fields whose classical actions
S_{\pm}[\varphi_{\pm}]
are evaluated subject to contour rotations
\tau\rightarrow\tau(1\mp i\epsilon)
respectively.
Both path integrals run over bulk field configurations subject to a common late-time boundary condition

\lim_{\tau\rightarrow 0^{-}}\Big[(-\tau)^{\Delta-d}\varphi_{\pm}(\tau,\bm{x})\Big]=\varphi_{(0)}(\bm{x}).

(107)

Performing a second path integral over the boundary field
\varphi_{(0)}(\bm{x})
then yields the de Sitter correlators as previously,

\displaystyle\langle\varphi_{(0)}(\bm{x}_{1})\ldots\varphi_{(0)}(\bm{x}_{n})\rangle

\displaystyle=\lim_{\tau\rightarrow 0^{-}}\Big[(-\tau)^{n(\Delta-d)}\langle\varphi(\tau,\bm{x}_{1})\ldots\varphi(\tau,\bm{x}_{n})\rangle\Big]

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{D}\varphi_{(0)}\,\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\varphi_{(0)}(\bm{x}_{i})\Big)\big|\Psi[\varphi_{(0)}]\big|^{2}.

(108)

In the Schwinger-Keldysh formalism these separate path integrals over bulk and boundary fields are merged into a single closed-time path integral

\displaystyle\langle\varphi(\tau,\bm{x}_{1})\ldots\varphi(\tau,\bm{x}_{n})\rangle=\int\mathcal{D}\varphi_{+}\mathcal{D}\varphi_{-}\,\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\varphi_{+}(\tau,\bm{x}_{i})\Big)\exp\Big(iS_{+}[\varphi_{+}]-iS_{-}[\varphi_{-}]\Big),

(109)

where we path integrate over all field configurations subject only to the constraint that both fields coincide at late times

\lim_{\tau\rightarrow 0^{-}}\varphi_{+}(\tau,\bm{x})=\lim_{\tau\rightarrow 0^{-}}\varphi_{-}(\tau,\bm{x}).

(110)

In this closed-time path formalism
\varphi_{+}
then corresponds to a field localised on the forward part of the contour while
\varphi_{-}
is localised on the reverse part.
As previously, the asymptotic time-dependence of the correlator can be removed by multiplying by a factor of
(-\tau)^{n(\Delta-d)}
and (in the absence of divergences) taking the limit
\tau\rightarrow 0^{-}
.

From the Schwinger-Keldysh perspective, we now obtain four different propagators according to the identity
\varphi_{\pm}
of the two end-points, and vertices come in two different types (see
Chen:2017ryl
for a review).
The pay-off for this increase in complexity is that the resulting diagrammatic expansion now computes correlators in de Sitter directly, in contrast to the wavefunction formalism where an additional path integral over boundary fields is required to go from the wavefunction to the correlators.

In this section we show how the holographic formulae derived above using the wavefunction formalism can also be obtained by directly continuing diagrams in the Schwinger-Keldysh formalism.
In particular, this requires continuing
\varphi_{\pm}
in different directions
Sleight:2020obc
;
Sleight:2021plv
. However, the derivation of the holographic formulae is arguably less straightforward in this approach since, as noted by many authors
Sleight:2020obc
;
Sleight:2021plv
;
DiPietro:2021sjt
, the AdS bulk-bulk propagator does not continue directly to the Schwinger-Keldysh bulk-bulk propagators. In contrast, the AdS bulk-bulk propagator does continue directly to the bulk-bulk propagator used for computing the wavefunction coefficients as we saw above. For this reason, we will restrict the Schwinger-Keldysh analysis in this section to tree-level correlators, unlike the wavefunction derivation which applied to all orders in perturbation theory.

3.1
Amplitudes

Restricting our analysis to tree level enables some further simplification of the holographic formulae (
102
)-(
104
). At tree level each correlator in the dual theory can be expressed as a sum of AdS Witten digrams multiplied by a number of constants, such as coupling constants, the AdS radius and the Planck length. By
AdS amplitudes
we refer to the momentum-dependent part of each Witten diagram, with all couplings dropped (or set to one). In
Bzowski:2022rlz
we defined and listed expressions for a number of interesting AdS amplitudes. Figure
1
shows the notation used for each amplitude and its defining Witten diagram.

\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}_{1}(\bm{k}_{1})
\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}_{3}(\bm{k}_{3})
\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}_{2}(\bm{k}_{2})
\scriptstyle\mathcal{K}_{[\Delta_{1}]}
\scriptstyle\mathcal{K}_{[\Delta_{3}]}
\scriptstyle\mathcal{K}_{[\Delta_{2}]}

(a)

Witten diagram for the

3-point amplitude
i_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}
.

\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}_{1}(\bm{k}_{1})
\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}_{2}(\bm{k}_{2})
\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}_{3}(\bm{k}_{3})
\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}_{4}(\bm{k}_{4})
\scriptstyle\mathcal{K}_{[\Delta_{1}]}
\scriptstyle\mathcal{K}_{[\Delta_{2}]}
\scriptstyle\mathcal{K}_{[\Delta_{3}]}
\scriptstyle\mathcal{K}_{[\Delta_{4}]}

(b)

The contact 4-point

amplitude
i_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}
.

\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}_{1}(\bm{k}_{1})
\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}_{2}(\bm{k}_{2})
\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}_{3}(\bm{k}_{3})
\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}_{4}(\bm{k}_{4})
\scriptstyle\mathcal{K}_{[\Delta_{1}]}
\scriptstyle\mathcal{K}_{[\Delta_{2}]}
\scriptstyle\mathcal{K}_{[\Delta_{3}]}
\scriptstyle\mathcal{K}_{[\Delta_{4}]}
\scriptstyle\mathcal{G}_{[\Delta_{x}]}

(c)

The exchange 4-point

amplitude
i_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}
.

Figure 1
:
AdS Witten diagrams defining the corresponding AdS amplitudes. We use
\mathcal{O}_{i}
to denote the dual operators, while
\mathcal{K}_{[\Delta]}
and
\mathcal{G}_{[\Delta]}
denote the bulk-to-boundary and bulk-to-bulk propagators respectively. For precise expressions defining these amplitudes, see section 3 of
Bzowski:2022rlz
.

In our conventions, every AdS diagram carries an overall factor
8
8

8

Note (
111
) differs from the usual expectation that diagrams scale as
(L_{AdS}/\ell_{P}^{(AdS)})^{(d-1)(1-L)}
. However, the latter holds when the interaction potential is
L_{AdS}^{-2}V_{int}(\varphi)
rather than

(\ell_{P}^{(AdS)})^{-2}V_{int}(\varphi)
as here.

\Big(\frac{L_{AdS}}{\ell_{P}^{(AdS)}}\Big)^{(d+1)V+(1-d)I}=\Big(\frac{L_{AdS}}{\ell_{P}^{(AdS)}}\Big)^{(d-1)(1-L)+2V},

(111)

where
L
is the number of loops,
V
the number of vertices and
I
the number of internal lines.
The power
(d-1)(1-L)
derives from the overall factor of
(L_{AdS}/\ell_{P}^{(AdS)})^{d-1}
multiplying the bulk action (
2.1
), just as an ordinary Feynman diagram in QFT is of order
\hbar^{L-1}=(\hbar^{-1})^{1-L}
since the action carries a factor of
\hbar^{-1}
in the path integral. The remaining power of
2V
arises since the interaction potential in (
2.1
) carries a factor of
(L_{AdS}/\ell_{P}^{(AdS)})^{2}
and we get one such factor per diagrammatic vertex. Thus, we can decompose each correlator in terms of AdS amplitudes. Schematically, we can write

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}(\bm{q}_{1})\ldots\mathcal{O}(\bm{q}_{n})\rangle\!\rangle=\sum_{V}\Big(\frac{L_{AdS}}{\ell_{P}^{(AdS)}}\Big)^{(d-1)+2V}\lambda_{AdS}^{V}\,i_{V}(\bm{q}_{1},\ldots,\bm{q}_{n}).

(112)

Here
i_{V}
represents a single Witten diagrams with
n
external legs and
V
vertices in the bulk. There is usually many such diagrams and they must be summed over. Similarly, each vertex comes with a coupling, and there may be many types of vertices/couplings. For simplicity we use only a single coupling here, denoted by
\lambda_{AdS}
.

Analogously, we can define the dS amplitudes, but carrying out the corresponding, tree-level calculations in de Sitter space. Thus, we can decompose the dS correlators as

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\varphi_{(0)}(\bm{q}_{1})\ldots\varphi_{(0)}(\bm{q}_{n})\rangle\!\rangle=\sum_{V}\Big(\frac{L_{dS}}{\ell_{P}^{(dS)}}\Big)^{(d-1)+2V}\lambda_{dS}^{V}\,ds_{V}(\bm{q}_{1},\ldots,\bm{q}_{n}).

(113)

We can now use the formulae (
102
)-(
104
) to relate the dS and AdS amplitudes on the tree level.

In (A)dS units
L_{(A)dS}=1
,
we see now that the continuation
\ell_{P}^{(AdS)}\rightarrow-i\ell_{P}^{(dS)}
generates a factor of
(-i)^{(1-d)(1-L)-2V}
for a given diagram. At tree level (
L=0
), this reduces to
-i(-i)^{-d}(-1)^{V}
and the factor of
(-i)^{-d}
cancels that in (
2.3.2
).
Thus in effect we can continue just the momentum alone:

\displaystyle\re\psi_{n}^{tree}(\bm{q}_{1},\ldots,\bm{q}_{n})=(-1)^{V}\im\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}(\bm{q}_{1})\ldots\mathcal{O}(\bm{q}_{n})\rangle\!\rangle\Big|_{q_{AdS}\rightarrow iq_{dS}}

(114)

The factor of
(-1)^{V}
can then be introduced into the holographic formulae explicitly, since at tree level
V=1
for contact diagrams and
V=2
for 4-point exchanges. All in all, at tree level, we can continue only the amplitudes, keeping all remaining constants identified
9
9

9

Alternatively, this factor of
(-1)^{V}
could be generated by continuing the couplings
\lambda^{AdS}_{k}\rightarrow-\lambda_{k}^{dS}
.
,

\displaystyle L_{AdS}=L_{dS},

\displaystyle\ell_{P}^{(AdS)}=\ell_{P}^{(dS)},

\displaystyle\lambda_{k}^{AdS}=\lambda_{k}^{dS}.

(115)

The relation between the amplitudes becomes then

\displaystyle ds_{[\Delta\Delta]}(q)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\im i_{[\Delta\Delta]}(\mathrm{i}q)},

(116)

\displaystyle ds_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}\frac{\im i_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}(\mathrm{i}q_{1},\mathrm{i}q_{2},\mathrm{i}q_{3})}{\prod_{j=1}^{3}\im i_{[\Delta_{j}\Delta_{j}]}(\mathrm{i}q_{j})},

(117)

\displaystyle ds_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}(q_{i})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{8}\frac{\im i_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}(\mathrm{i}q_{i})}{\prod_{j=1}^{4}\im i_{[\Delta_{j}\Delta_{j}]}(\mathrm{i}q_{j})},

(118)

\displaystyle ds_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}(q_{i},s)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}\prod_{j=1}^{4}\frac{1}{\im i_{[\Delta_{j}\Delta_{j}]}(\mathrm{i}q_{j})}\left[\im i_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}(\mathrm{i}q_{i},\mathrm{i}s)\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\left.-\,\frac{\im i_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{x}]}(\mathrm{i}q_{1},\mathrm{i}q_{2},\mathrm{i}s)\im i_{[\Delta_{x}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}(\mathrm{i}s,\mathrm{i}q_{3},\mathrm{i}q_{4})}{\im i_{[\Delta_{x}\Delta_{x}]}(\mathrm{i}s)}\right],

(119)

where
i_{[\Delta\Delta]}(q)
is the regulated AdS 2-point function;
i_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})
the AdS 3-point function;
i_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3},q_{4})
the AdS 4-point contact and
i_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}(q_{j},s)
the AdS 4-point
s
-channel exchange diagram for a field of dimension
\Delta_{x}
.
As
L_{(A)dS}
and
\ell_{P}^{(A)dS}
are no longer being continued, we can effectively set
L_{(A)dS}=1
and
\ell_{P}^{(A)dS}=1
, with the only analytic continuations being those of the momenta indicated directly in the formulae.

3.2
Free field

Let us begin with the analysis of the free scalar field
\varphi
governed by the action,

\displaystyle S_{0}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}^{d+1}x\sqrt{-g}\left[g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\varphi\partial_{\nu}\varphi+m_{dS}^{2}\varphi^{2}\right],

(120)

on the fixed
(d+1)
-dimensional de Sitter background with the metric

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}s^{2}=\frac{1}{(-\tau)^{2}}(-\mathrm{d}\tau^{2}+\mathrm{d}\bm{x}^{2}),

(121)

where
\tau
goes from
-\infty
to
0
. We parameterise the mass as

\displaystyle m_{dS}^{2}=\frac{d^{2}}{4}-\beta^{2}=-\Delta(\Delta-d)

(122)

and consider only the case of
0<\beta\leq\frac{d}{2}
.

This is the free part of the system described by the de Sitter action (
50
) with
L_{dS}=\ell_{P}^{(dS)}=1
. One can recover the
L_{dS}
and
\ell_{P}^{(dS)}
dependence by decorating each Witten diagram (amplitude) with the factor (
111
), where
V
denotes the number of vertices in the diagram (and we consider only tree diagrams with
L=0
). In particular by stripping the 2-point function
\langle\!\langle\varphi_{(0)}\varphi_{(0)}\rangle\!\rangle
from its
L_{dS}
and
\ell_{P}^{(dS)}
we obtain the dS amplitude
ds_{[\Delta\Delta]}
as its purely momentum-dependent part,

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\varphi_{(0)}(\bm{q})\varphi_{(0)}(-\bm{q})\rangle\!\rangle=\Big(\frac{L_{dS}}{\ell^{(dS)}_{P}}\Big)^{d-1}ds_{[\Delta\Delta]}(q)+O(\lambda_{j}).

(123)

If we added some bulk interactions, denoted here collectively by the coupling constants
\lambda_{j}
, the 2-point function would acquire additional contributions from bulk loops. Nevertheless, by
ds_{[\Delta\Delta]}
we always denote the zeroth order, tree-level amplitude; in this case the free theory amplitude.

3.2.1
Mode decomposition

We write the mode decomposition

\displaystyle\varphi(\tau,\bm{x})=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{q}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\left[a_{\bm{q}}\varphi_{\bm{q}}(\tau,\bm{x})+a^{\ast}_{\bm{q}}\varphi^{\ast}_{\bm{q}}(\tau,\bm{x})\right],

(124)

where each modes satisfies the Klein-Gordon equation
(-\Box+m_{dS}^{2})\varphi_{\bm{q}}=0
. The negative frequency solution
\varphi_{\bm{q}}
reads

\displaystyle\varphi_{\bm{q}}(\tau,\bm{x})

\displaystyle=e^{\mathrm{i}\bm{q}\cdot\bm{x}}\varphi_{\bm{q}}(\tau),

(125)

\displaystyle\varphi_{\bm{q}}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2}e^{\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi}{2}(\beta+\frac{1}{2})}(-\tau)^{\frac{d}{2}}H^{(1)}_{\beta}(-q\tau),

(126)

where
H^{(1)}_{\beta}
denotes the Hankel function and we chose the phase such that in the far past

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\rightarrow-\infty}\varphi_{\bm{q}}(\tau)\sim(-\tau)^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\frac{e^{-\mathrm{i}q\tau}}{\sqrt{2q}}.

(127)

The normalisation is such that with respect to the Klein-Gordon scalar product

\displaystyle(\varphi,\psi)=-\mathrm{i}(-\tau)^{1-d}\int\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{x}\left[\varphi\,\partial_{\tau}\psi^{\ast}-\partial_{\tau}\varphi\>\psi^{\ast}\right]

(128)

these modes are normalised as

\displaystyle(\varphi_{\bm{q}},\varphi_{\bm{q}^{\prime}})=(2\pi)^{d}\delta(\bm{q}-\bm{q}^{\prime}).

(129)

The integral is taken over any constant-
\tau
surface and is independent of the choice of
\tau
. This tells us that the coefficients in (
124
) are canonically normalised creation-annihilation operators after quantisation. The vacuum state,
|0\rangle
, annihilated by all
a_{\bm{q}}
, is the vacuum state in far past where the modes behave as in (
127
).

3.2.2
Propagators

The Wightman functions
G_{-+}
and
G_{+-}
are defined as

\displaystyle G_{-+}(x_{1},x_{2})=\langle 0|\varphi(x_{1})\varphi(x_{2})|0\rangle_{0},

\displaystyle G_{+-}(x_{1},x_{2})=\langle 0|\varphi(x_{2})\varphi(x_{1})|0\rangle_{0},

(130)

where the subscript
\langle-\rangle_{0}
reminds us that the expectation value is evaluated in the free theory. In momentum space the overall delta function drops out,

\displaystyle G_{\mp\pm}(\tau_{1},\bm{q}_{1};\tau_{2},\bm{q}_{2})=(2\pi)^{d}\delta(\bm{q}_{1}+\bm{q}_{2})G_{\mp\pm}(q_{1},\tau_{1},\tau_{2}),

(131)

and we obtain

\displaystyle G_{+-}(q,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=\varphi^{\ast}_{\bm{q}}(\tau_{1})\varphi_{-\bm{q}}(\tau_{2})=\frac{\pi}{4}(-\tau_{1})^{d/2}(-\tau_{2})^{d/2}H^{(2)}_{\beta}(-q\tau_{1})H^{(1)}_{\beta}(-q\tau_{2})

(132)

\displaystyle G_{-+}(q,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=\varphi_{\bm{q}}(\tau_{1})\varphi^{\ast}_{-\bm{q}}(\tau_{2})=G_{+-}^{\ast}(q,\tau_{1},\tau_{2}),

(133)

The time-ordered propagator
G_{++}
and the anti-time-ordered propagator
G_{--}
are

\displaystyle G_{++}(q,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=\langle\!\langle 0|T\left[\varphi(\tau_{1},\bm{q})\varphi(\tau_{2},-\bm{q})\right]|0\rangle\!\rangle_{0}

\displaystyle=\Theta(\tau_{1}-\tau_{2})G_{-+}(q,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\Theta(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})G_{+-}(q,\tau_{1},\tau_{2}),

(134)

\displaystyle G_{--}(q,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=\langle\!\langle 0|\bar{T}\left[\varphi(\tau_{1},\bm{q})\varphi(\tau_{2},-\bm{q})\right]|0\rangle\!\rangle_{0}

\displaystyle=\Theta(\tau_{1}-\tau_{2})G_{+-}(q,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\Theta(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})G_{-+}(q,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=G_{++}^{\ast}(q,\tau_{1},\tau_{2}).

(135)

This agrees with both the conventions of
Birrell:1982ix
and
Chen:2017ryl
. Note that these propagators are invariant under
\beta\to-\beta
, as follows from
H^{(1)}_{-\beta}=e^{\beta\pi i}H^{(1)}_{\beta}
and
H^{(2)}_{-\beta}=e^{-\beta\pi i}H^{(2)}_{\beta}
.

The asymptotic behaviour of the free field near the boundary at
\tau=0
is given by (
53
). Thus, the bulk-to-boundary propagators are defined as leading terms in the expansion of
G_{++}
near the boundary,

\displaystyle G_{+}(q,\tau)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\tau_{0}\rightarrow 0^{-}}G_{++}(q,\tau,\tau_{0})=\lim_{\tau_{0}\rightarrow 0^{-}}G_{+-}(q,\tau,\tau_{0})

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}(-\tau_{0})^{\frac{d}{2}-\beta}2^{\beta-2}\Gamma(\beta)q^{-\beta}(-\tau)^{\frac{d}{2}}H^{(2)}_{\beta}(-q\tau),

(136)

\displaystyle G_{-}(q,\tau)

\displaystyle=\left[G_{+}(q,\tau)\right]^{\ast}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}(-\tau_{0})^{\frac{d}{2}-\beta}2^{\beta-2}\Gamma(\beta)q^{-\beta}(-\tau)^{\frac{d}{2}}H^{(1)}_{\beta}(-q\tau).

(137)

It is important here that this expression is valid for all
\beta>0
. In particular there are no logarithmic terms when
\beta
is integral. This follows from the definition of the Hankel functions as
H^{(\pm)}_{\beta}=J_{\beta}\pm\mathrm{i}Y_{\beta}
with
+
for
H^{(1)}
and
-
for
H^{(2)}
and the power expansions of the Bessel functions involved.

3.2.3
2-point function

We can calculate the free late time 2-point function in the canonical formalism. The 2-point amplitude,
i.e.
, its momentum-dependent part, free of the constants
L_{dS}
and
\ell_{P}^{(dS)}
reads

\displaystyle ds_{[\Delta\Delta]}(q)

\displaystyle=\langle\!\langle 0|\varphi_{(0)}(\bm{q})\varphi_{(0)}(-\bm{q})|0\rangle\!\rangle_{0}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\tau_{0}\rightarrow 0^{-}}\left[(-\tau_{0})^{2(d-\Delta)}\langle\!\langle 0|\varphi(\tau_{0},\bm{q})\varphi(\tau_{0},-\bm{q})|0\rangle\!\rangle_{0}\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{\tau_{0}\rightarrow 0^{-}}\left[(-\tau_{0})^{2(d-\Delta)}\varphi_{\bm{q}}(\tau_{0})\varphi^{\ast}_{-\bm{q}}(\tau_{0})\right]

\displaystyle=a_{\beta}q^{-2\beta},

(138)

where we defined the constant

\displaystyle a_{\beta}=\frac{4^{\beta-1}\Gamma^{2}(\beta)}{\pi}.

(139)

This constant will appear repeatedly throughout the calculations. In order to stay consistent with the notation for the AdS amplitudes in
Bzowski:2022rlz
we use the conformal weights
\Delta
to distinguish various de Sitter amplitudes. The relations between
\beta
,
\Delta
and de Sitter mass-squared
m_{dS}^{2}
are given by

\displaystyle\beta=\Delta-\frac{d}{2},

\displaystyle m_{dS}^{2}L_{dS}^{2}=-\Delta(\Delta-d)=\frac{d^{2}}{4}-\beta^{2}.

(140)

We are mostly interested in cases
\Delta=2
and
3
in
d=3
boundary spacetime dimensions, which correspond to
\beta=1/2
and
3/2
. From the point of view of the
4
-dimensional de Sitter bulk, these correspond to the conformally coupled (
m_{dS}^{2}L_{dS}^{2}=2
) and massless (
m_{dS}^{2}L_{dS}^{2}=0
) dual scalar fields respectively.

Clearly, the 2-point function matches the late-time limits of the propagators,

\displaystyle ds_{[\Delta\Delta]}(q)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\tau_{0}\rightarrow 0^{-}}(-\tau_{0})^{2(d-\Delta)}G_{++}(q,\tau_{0},\tau_{0})

\displaystyle=\lim_{\tau_{0}\rightarrow 0^{-}}(-\tau_{0})^{2(d-\Delta)}G_{-+}(q,\tau_{0},\tau_{0})

\displaystyle=\lim_{\tau_{0}\rightarrow 0^{-}}(-\tau_{0})^{2(d-\Delta)}G_{+}(q,\tau_{0}).

(141)

As discussed in section
3.2.2
, these results are valid for any real, positive
\beta
. In particular the dS 2-point function is always of the form
q^{-2\beta}
and there are
no logarithmic terms
present when
\beta
is an integer.

The 2-point amplitude can be rewritten as

\displaystyle ds_{[\Delta\Delta]}(q)=-\frac{1}{2\sin(\pi\beta)i_{[\Delta\Delta]}(q)},

(142)

where

\displaystyle i_{[\Delta\Delta]}(q)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\Gamma(1-\beta)}{2^{2\beta-1}\Gamma(\beta)}q^{2\beta}=-\frac{q^{2\beta}}{2\sin(\pi\beta)a_{\beta}}.

(143)

is the properly normalised AdS 2-point function. For integral
\beta
’s the expression (
142
) must be understood as a limit. The singularity in the AdS 2-point function
i_{[\Delta\Delta]}
is then cancelled by the sine in (
142
) and a finite dS 2-point function of the form
q^{-2\beta}
is obtained for all
\beta>0
. One can hide the sine even further by writing

\displaystyle ds_{[\Delta\Delta]}(q)=-\frac{1}{2\im i_{[\Delta\Delta]}(\mathrm{i}q)}.

(144)

Now, however, the meaning of this expression for integral
\beta
’s is obscured. For integral betas this is still understood as the limit. Consequently, the dS 2-point function is immune to any logarithmic terms or renormalisation effects in the AdS 2-point function.

3.3
Interactions, Schwinger-Keldysh and the generating functions

Let us now consider the system in the presence of interactions,
S=S_{0}+S_{int}
, where
S_{0}
is the free action (
120
). We are interested in the boundary correlation functions with all operator insertions at the boundary at
\tau_{0}=0
. We define
\varphi_{(0)}
as the leading term in the expansion (
53
),

\displaystyle\varphi_{(0)}(\bm{x})=\lim_{\tau_{0}\rightarrow 0}\left[(-\tau_{0})^{\Delta-d}\varphi(\tau,\bm{x})\right].

(145)

In the presence of interactions
\varphi_{(0)}
remains well-defined except in the case of a massless particle,
m^{2}_{dS}=0
, which corresponds to
\beta=d/2
. In such a case additional logarithmic terms,
\log^{j}(-\tau)
, appear in (
145
), with
j
denoting the order of the perturbative expansion as in (
39
). In this paper, however, as in its AdS counterpart,
Bzowski:2022rlz
, we work within the framework of dimensional regularisation and renormalisation. We treat dimensions
d
and
\Delta
as parameters and treat amplitudes (Witten diagrams) and correlation functions as their functions. We assume a generic near-boundary expansion (
53
) and derive the amplitudes. As the obtained amplitudes are valid in some open, non-empty set of the dimensions
d
and
\Delta
, one can use analytic continuation to define them for almost all other values of dimensions. On the other hand, features such as the presence of secular, logarithmic terms in the near-boundary expansions is signaled by divergences (poles) for special values of the dimensions, such as
\Delta=d
or, equivalently,
\beta=d/2
. For this reason, for now, we will consider the generic case of the expansion (
53
) and then carry out the renormalisation procedure in section
4
.

With
\varphi_{(0)}
defined in (
145
), we define the cosmological correlators as

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\varphi_{(0)}(\bm{q}_{1})\ldots\varphi_{(0)}(\bm{q}_{n})\rangle\!\rangle=\lim_{\tau_{0}\rightarrow 0^{-}}(-\tau_{0})^{\Delta_{t}-nd}\langle\!\langle\varphi(\tau_{0},\bm{q}_{1})\ldots\varphi(\tau_{0},\bm{q}_{n})\rangle\!\rangle,

(146)

where
\Delta_{t}
is the sum of dimensions associated with
\varphi
. Here, for simplicity, we take all fields to be identical (so
\Delta_{t}=n\Delta
)
in the interacting theory, evaluated perturbatively in the couplings
\lambda_{3}
and
\lambda_{4}
. According to
Chen:2017ryl
this can be done utilising the Schwinger-Keldysh formalism. The correlator is expressed as

\displaystyle\langle\varphi(0,\bm{q}_{1})\ldots\varphi(0,\bm{q}_{n})\rangle=\int\mathcal{D}\varphi_{+}\mathcal{D}\varphi_{-}\>\varphi_{+}(0,\bm{q}_{1})\ldots\varphi_{+}(0,\bm{q}_{n})\exp\left(\mathrm{i}S_{+}[\varphi_{+}]-\mathrm{i}S_{-}[\varphi_{-}]\right),

(147)

where the path integrals are taken over fields
\varphi_{-}
and
\varphi_{+}
whose values match at
\tau_{0}=0
. The actions
S_{+}
and
S_{-}
are identical with the full action
S
, except that now specific integration contours are specified,

\displaystyle S^{int}_{\pm}[\varphi]=-\int_{-\infty(1\mp\mathrm{i}\epsilon)}^{0}\mathrm{d}\tau\int\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{x}\sqrt{-g}\,L[\varphi].

(148)

The path integral on the right-hand side of (
147
) can be thought of as evaluated in the auxiliary Schwinger-Keldysh theory represented by the partition function,

\displaystyle Z[J_{+},J_{-}]

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{D}\varphi_{+}\mathcal{D}\varphi_{-}\exp\left[\mathrm{i}S_{+}[\varphi_{+}]-\mathrm{i}S_{-}[\varphi_{-}]+\mathrm{i}\int\mathrm{d}^{d+1}x\sqrt{-g}\left(J_{+}\varphi_{+}-J_{-}\varphi_{-}\right)\right].

(149)

This allows us to apply to (
147
) standard perturbation theory, Wick contractions
etc.
, with the exception that we have now four propagators,

\displaystyle\langle\varphi_{\sigma_{1}}(x_{1})\varphi_{\sigma_{2}}(x_{2})\rangle_{SK}

\displaystyle=\frac{-\mathrm{i}\sigma_{1}}{\sqrt{-g(x_{1})}}\frac{\delta}{\delta J_{\sigma_{1}}(x_{1})}\frac{-\mathrm{i}\sigma_{2}}{\sqrt{-g(x_{2})}}\frac{\delta}{\delta J_{\sigma_{2}}(x_{2})}Z[J_{+},J_{-}]

\displaystyle=G_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}(x_{1},x_{2})

(150)

according to the choice of signs
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}=\pm
.

3.4
3-point function

Consider three scalar fields
\varphi_{j}
for
j=1,2,3
governed by the action

\displaystyle S_{3}

\displaystyle=S_{0}+S_{int},

(151)

\displaystyle S_{0}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}^{d}x\sqrt{-g}\sum_{j=1,2,3}\left[\partial_{\mu}\varphi_{j}\partial^{\mu}\varphi_{j}+m^{2}_{j}\varphi_{j}^{2}\right],

(152)

\displaystyle S_{int}

\displaystyle=-\lambda_{123}\int\mathrm{d}^{d}x\sqrt{-g}\,\varphi_{1}\varphi_{2}\varphi_{3},

(153)

where
\lambda_{123}
is a coupling constant. The boundary 3-point function can be presented in the form (
113
), which in this case becomes

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\varphi_{1(0)}(\bm{q}_{1})\varphi_{2(0)}(\bm{q}_{2})\varphi_{3(0)}(\bm{q}_{3})\rangle\!\rangle=\Big(\frac{L_{dS}}{\ell_{P}^{(dS)}}\Big)^{d+1}\lambda_{123}ds_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})+O(\lambda_{123}^{2}).

(154)

By
ds_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}
we denote a momentum-dependent amplitude represented by the sum of the tree-level Witten diagrams presented in figure
2
. In general, the 3-point functions receives bulk loop corrections, all of higher order in the coupling
\lambda_{123}
.

+
\varphi_{1(0)}
\varphi_{2(0)}
\varphi_{3(0)}
G_{+}^{[\Delta_{1}]}
G_{+}^{[\Delta_{2}]}
G_{+}^{[\Delta_{3}]}
+
-
\varphi_{1(0)}
\varphi_{2(0)}
\varphi_{3(0)}
G_{-}^{[\Delta_{1}]}
G_{-}^{[\Delta_{2}]}
G_{-}^{[\Delta_{3}]}

Figure 2
:
Witten diagrams contributing to the de Sitter amplitude
ds_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}
. The blue line denotes the boundary surface of
\tau_{0}=0
.

In order to evaluate it, notice that the formula (
147
) produces two terms, one from the expansion of
S_{+}
and one from
S_{-}
. The two terms are complex conjugates of each other and we can think about them as obtained from the two vertices

\displaystyle V_{\pm}[f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}]=\mp\mathrm{i}\lambda_{123}\int_{-\infty(1\mp\mathrm{i}\epsilon)}^{0}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau}{(-\tau)^{d+1}}f_{1}(\tau)f_{2}(\tau)f_{3}(\tau).

(155)

The Schwinger-Keldysh formalism (
147
) tells us that we are allowed to plug only
+
edges into
V_{+}
and
-
edges into
G_{-}
. With
\tau_{0}=0
this means that

\displaystyle ds_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})=2\re\left[-\mathrm{i}\int_{-\infty(1-\mathrm{i}\epsilon)}^{0}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau}{(-\tau)^{d+1}}G^{[\Delta_{1}]}_{+}(q_{1},\tau)G^{[\Delta_{2}]}_{+}(q_{2},\tau)G_{+}^{[\Delta_{3}]}(q_{3},\tau)\right],

(156)

with
G^{[\Delta]}_{+}
denoting the propagator (
3.2.2
) for the field whose mass is parameterised by
\Delta
.

There is an important assumption here, that the integral converges at the upper limit of
\tau=0
. In other words that we can take the
\tau_{0}\rightarrow 0^{-}
limit before evaluating the integral. In such a case the most leading term in
\tau_{0}
simply follows from the explicit factors in (
3.2.2
) and (
137
) and for the 3-point function is
(-\tau_{0})^{\frac{3d}{2}-\beta_{t}}=(-\tau_{0})^{3d-\Delta_{t}}
, where
\beta_{t}=\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\beta_{3}
and
\Delta_{t}=\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{3}
. If the integral diverges at
\tau=0
, however, there are more leading terms, which can be extracted from the near boundary behaviour of the integrand. For now we assume that all integrals converge.

Just as in Minkowski field theory the integrals present in Feynman diagrams are carried out over slightly imaginary contours. In our case these contours are specified in (
148
). Whether the contour goes over a slightly positive or negative imaginary times determines the direction of the contour rotation as presented in figure
3
. For
S_{+}
, the contour runs over slightly imaginary, positive times
\tau
, and thus it can be deformed to the contour running from
+\mathrm{i}\infty
to
0
. Similarly, for
S_{-}
the contour gets deformed to the contour running from
-\mathrm{i}\infty
to
0
. Thus, we substitute

\displaystyle\text{for }+

\displaystyle\tau=\mathrm{i}z,

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty(1-\mathrm{i}\epsilon)}^{0}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau}{(-\tau)^{d+1}}=e^{\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi d}{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z^{d+1}},

(157)

\displaystyle\text{for }-

\displaystyle\tau=-\mathrm{i}z,

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty(1+\mathrm{i}\epsilon)}^{0}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau}{(-\tau)^{d+1}}=e^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi d}{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z^{d+1}}.

(158)

This correspond to how Hankel functions are continued; both
H^{(1)}_{\beta}
and
H^{(2)}_{\beta}
continue to the Bessel-
K
functions

\displaystyle H^{(1)}_{\beta}(\mathrm{i}x)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\mathrm{i}}{\pi}e^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi\beta}{2}}K_{\beta}(x),

\displaystyle H^{(2)}_{\beta}(-\mathrm{i}x)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\mathrm{i}}{\pi}e^{\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi\beta}{2}}K_{\beta}(x).

(159)

valid for real
x>0
and real
\beta
. In this way we find

\displaystyle G_{\pm}(q,\pm\mathrm{i}z)=e^{\pm\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi}{2}(\beta-\frac{d}{2})}(-\tau_{0})^{\frac{d}{2}-\beta}a_{\beta}q^{-2\beta}\mathcal{K}_{\beta}(q,z)

(160)

where
a_{\beta}
is defined in (
139
) and

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\beta}(q,z)

\displaystyle=\frac{q^{\beta}z^{\frac{d}{2}}K_{\beta}(qz)}{2^{\beta-1}\Gamma(\beta)}.

(161)

Here we can think of
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}
as the AdS bulk-to-boundary propagator. Just as for the 2-point function, these expressions are valid for all
\beta>0
. Note that all factors except the explicit exponent are real.

\tau
z
\tau
z

Figure 3
:
Deformations of the two contours running from
\tau=-\infty
to
\tau=0
to two the contours running over the imaginary axis. On the left the original dotted contour runs with slightly positive imaginary part, while the contour on the right has a slightly negative imaginary part. The arcs in the middle correspond to the split of the integration limits according to whether
\tau_{1}<\tau_{2}
or the opposite hold. Note that if the cut-off regularisation is imposed close to zero, one should also consider a small additional arc there.

Going back to the integral (
156
) we apply the analytic continuation to find

\displaystyle ds_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})

\displaystyle\qquad=2\lim_{\tau_{0}\rightarrow 0^{-}}(-\tau_{0})^{\Delta_{t}-3d}\im\left[e^{\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi d}{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z^{d+1}}G_{+}(q_{1},\mathrm{i}z)G_{+}(q_{2},\mathrm{i}z)G_{+}(q_{3},\mathrm{i}z)\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=2\prod_{j=1}^{3}\left[q_{j}^{-2\beta_{j}}a_{\beta_{j}}\right]\im\exp\left[\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi}{2}\left(\beta_{t}-\frac{d}{2}\right)\right]\times

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z^{d+1}}\mathcal{K}_{\beta_{1}}(q_{1},z)\mathcal{K}_{\beta_{2}}(q_{2},z)\mathcal{K}_{\beta_{3}}(q_{3},z)

\displaystyle\qquad=2\sin\left[\frac{\pi}{2}(\beta_{t}-\frac{d}{2})\right]\prod_{j=1}^{3}\left[q_{j}^{-2\beta_{j}}a_{\beta_{j}}\right]i_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}),

(162)

where
i_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}
denotes the AdS amplitude. The combination under the sine reads

\displaystyle\beta_{t}-\frac{d}{2}=\Delta_{t}-2d,

(163)

the total dimension of the AdS amplitude. Thus for finite or regulated amplitudes one can substitute

\displaystyle\sin\left[\frac{\pi}{2}(\beta_{t}-\frac{d}{2})\right]i^{\text{reg}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})=\im i^{\text{reg}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}(\mathrm{i}q_{1},\mathrm{i}q_{2},\mathrm{i}q_{3}).

(164)

The 3-point function (
3.4
) is valid for any finite
\beta
’s including integral ones. We can express
a_{\beta}q^{-2\beta}
in terms of the AdS 2-point function by inverting (
144
)

\displaystyle a_{\beta}q^{-2\beta}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\im i^{\text{reg}}_{[\Delta\Delta]}(\mathrm{i}q)}.

(165)

The problem with this representation is that for integral
\beta
the formula suggests that some logarithmic terms appear due to the divergence of the 2-point function. As discussed in the previous section, this is not the case as
\im i^{\text{reg}}_{[\Delta\Delta]}(\mathrm{i}q)
is understood as the limit and is perfectly finite. Thus, we can consistently write the expression relating regulated AdS and dS amplitudes,

\displaystyle ds^{\text{reg}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})=-\frac{1}{4}\frac{\im i^{\text{reg}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}(\mathrm{i}q_{1},\mathrm{i}q_{2},\mathrm{i}q_{3})}{\prod_{j=1}^{3}\im i^{\text{reg}}_{[\Delta_{j}\Delta_{j}]}(\mathrm{i}q_{j})}.

(166)

3.5
4-point function

Consider now five scalar bulk fields
\varphi_{j}
for
j=1,2,3,4,x
governed by the action,

\displaystyle S_{4}

\displaystyle=S_{0}+S_{int},

(167)

\displaystyle S_{0}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}^{d}x\sqrt{-g}\sum_{j=1,2,3,4,x}\left[\partial_{\mu}\varphi_{j}\partial^{\mu}\varphi_{j}+m^{2}_{j}\varphi_{j}^{2}\right],

(168)

\displaystyle S_{int}

\displaystyle=-\int\mathrm{d}^{d}x\sqrt{-g}\left[\lambda_{12x}\varphi_{1}\varphi_{2}\varphi_{x}+\lambda_{34x}\varphi_{x}\varphi_{3}\varphi_{4}-\lambda_{1234}\varphi_{1}\varphi_{2}\varphi_{3}\varphi_{4}\right]

(169)

on the fixed
(d+1)
-dimensional de Sitter background (
121
). The idea behind is that such a theory contains as few symmetries as possible. In particular the leading terms in the 3-point functions
\langle\!\langle\varphi_{1}\varphi_{2}\varphi_{x}\rangle\!\rangle
and
\langle\!\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{3}\varphi_{4}\rangle\!\rangle
and the 4-point function
\langle\!\langle\varphi_{1}\varphi_{2}\varphi_{3}\varphi_{4}\rangle\!\rangle
are given by single Witten diagrams (amplitudes). According to (
113
) we have two amplitudes to consider: the contact 4-point amplitude with
V=1
, which we denote by
ds_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}
, and the exchange 4-point amplitude with
V=2
, which we denote by
ds_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}
. We have

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\varphi_{1(0)}(\bm{q}_{1})\varphi_{2(0)}(\bm{q}_{2})\varphi_{3(0)}(\bm{q}_{3})\varphi_{4(0)}(\bm{q}_{4})\rangle\!\rangle=\left(\frac{L_{dS}}{\ell_{P}^{(dS)}}\right)^{d+1}\lambda_{1234}ds_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3},q_{4})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\left(\frac{L_{dS}}{\ell_{P}^{(dS)}}\right)^{d+3}\lambda_{12x}\lambda_{34x}ds_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3},q_{4},s)+\ldots

(170)

where the dropped terms are the bulk loop corrections, all higher order in the couplings. The sign choices in the action (
169
) follow the equation of (4.7) in
Bzowski:2022rlz
, so that the dS and AdS interaction actions differ by the overall sign.

3.5.1
Contact diagram

The situation here is analogous to the case of the 3-point function. The contact 4-point diagram is depicted in figure
4
.

+
\varphi_{1(0)}
\varphi_{2(0)}
\varphi_{3(0)}
\varphi_{4(0)}
G_{+}^{[\Delta_{1}]}
G_{+}^{[\Delta_{2}]}
G_{+}^{[\Delta_{3}]}
G_{+}^{[\Delta_{4}]}
-
+
\varphi_{1(0)}
\varphi_{2(0)}
\varphi_{3(0)}
\varphi_{4(0)}
G_{-}^{[\Delta_{1}]}
G_{-}^{[\Delta_{2}]}
G_{-}^{[\Delta_{3}]}
G_{-}^{[\Delta_{4}]}

Figure 4
:
Witten diagrams contributing to the de Sitter amplitude
ds_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}
. The blue line denotes the late-time boundary
\tau_{0}=0
.

With the vertex

\displaystyle V_{\pm}[f_{1},f_{2},f_{3},f_{4}]

\displaystyle=\pm\mathrm{i}\lambda_{1234}\int_{-\infty(1\mp\mathrm{i}\epsilon)}^{0}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau}{(-\tau)^{d+1}}f_{1}(\tau)f_{2}(\tau)f_{3}(\tau)f_{4}(\tau)

(171)

and
\beta_{T}=\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\beta_{3}+\beta_{4}
we get

\displaystyle ds^{\text{fin}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3},q_{4})=

\displaystyle\qquad=2\lim_{\tau_{0}\rightarrow 0^{-}}(-\tau_{0})^{\Delta_{T}-4d}\re\left[\mathrm{i}\int_{-\infty(1-\mathrm{i}\epsilon)}^{0}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau}{(-\tau)^{d+1}}G_{+}(q_{1},\tau)G_{+}(q_{2},\tau)G_{+}(q_{3},\tau)G_{+}(q_{4},\tau)\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=-2\sin\left[\frac{\pi}{2}(\beta_{T}-d)\right]\prod_{j=1}^{4}\left[q_{j}^{-2\beta_{j}}a_{\beta_{j}}\right]i^{\text{fin}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3},q_{4})

\displaystyle\qquad=-\frac{1}{8}\frac{\im i^{\text{fin}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}(\mathrm{i}q_{j})}{\prod_{j=1}^{4}\im i^{\text{reg}}_{[\Delta_{j}\Delta_{j}]}(\mathrm{i}q_{j})}.

(172)

3.5.2
4-point exchange

The 4-point exchange amplitude is depicted in figure
5
. This amplitude possesses four terms arising from the four choices for the pairs
V_{\pm}
of the left and right vertex,

\displaystyle ds_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}=\lim_{\tau_{0}\rightarrow 0^{-}}(-\tau_{0})^{\Delta_{T}-4d}\left(I_{++}+I_{+-}+I_{-+}+I_{--}\right),

(173)

where

\displaystyle I_{++}

\displaystyle=V_{+}[G_{+}(q_{1}),G_{+}(q_{2}),V_{+}[G_{++}(s),G_{+}(q_{3}),G_{+}(q_{4})]]

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}\int_{-\infty(1-\mathrm{i}\epsilon)}^{0}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{1}}{(-\tau_{1})^{d+1}}G_{+}(q_{1},\tau_{1})G_{+}(q_{2},\tau_{1})\times

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times(-\mathrm{i})\int_{-\infty(1-\mathrm{i}\epsilon)}^{0}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{2}}{(-\tau_{2})^{d+1}}G_{++}(s,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})G_{+}(q_{3},\tau_{2})G_{+}(q_{4},\tau_{2}),

(174)

\displaystyle I_{+-}

\displaystyle=V_{+}[G_{+}(q_{1}),G_{+}(q_{2}),V_{-}[G_{+-}(s),G_{-}(q_{3}),G_{-}(q_{4})]]

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}\int_{-\infty(1-\mathrm{i}\epsilon)}^{0}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{1}}{(-\tau_{1})^{d+1}}G_{+}(q_{1},\tau_{1})G_{+}(q_{2},\tau_{1})\times

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\mathrm{i}\int_{-\infty(1+\mathrm{i}\epsilon)}^{0}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{2}}{(-\tau_{2})^{d+1}}G_{+-}(s,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})G_{-}(q_{3},\tau_{2})G_{-}(q_{4},\tau_{2}),

(175)

while
I_{--}=I_{++}^{\ast}
and
I_{-+}=I_{+-}^{\ast}
.

+
+
\varphi_{1(0)}
\varphi_{2(0)}
\varphi_{3(0)}
\varphi_{4(0)}
G_{+}^{[\Delta_{1}]}
G_{+}^{[\Delta_{2}]}
G_{+}^{[\Delta_{3}]}
G_{+}^{[\Delta_{4}]}
G_{++}^{[\Delta_{x}]}
+
-
-
\varphi_{1(0)}
\varphi_{2(0)}
\varphi_{3(0)}
\varphi_{4(0)}
G_{-}^{[\Delta_{1}]}
G_{-}^{[\Delta_{2}]}
G_{-}^{[\Delta_{3}]}
G_{-}^{[\Delta_{4}]}
G_{--}^{[\Delta_{x}]}
+
+
-
\varphi_{1(0)}
\varphi_{2(0)}
\varphi_{3(0)}
\varphi_{4(0)}
G_{+}^{[\Delta_{1}]}
G_{+}^{[\Delta_{2}]}
G_{-}^{[\Delta_{3}]}
G_{-}^{[\Delta_{4}]}
G_{+-}^{[\Delta_{x}]}
+
-
+
\varphi_{1(0)}
\varphi_{2(0)}
\varphi_{3(0)}
\varphi_{4(0)}
G_{-}^{[\Delta_{1}]}
G_{-}^{[\Delta_{2}]}
G_{+}^{[\Delta_{3}]}
G_{+}^{[\Delta_{4}]}
G_{-+}^{[\Delta_{x}]}

Figure 5
:
Witten diagrams contributing to the 4-point exchange de Sitter amplitude
ds_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}
. The blue line denotes the late-time boundary
\tau_{0}=0
.

In order to calculate the integrals in (
174
) and (
175
) we have to carry out the appropriate analytic continuations. The dS bulk-to-bulk propagators are

\displaystyle G_{+-}(q,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{4}(-\tau_{1})^{d/2}(-\tau_{2})^{d/2}H^{(2)}_{\beta}(-q\tau_{1})H^{(1)}_{\beta}(-q\tau_{2}),

(176)

\displaystyle G_{++}(q,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=G_{--}^{\ast}(q,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=G_{-+}(q,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\Theta(\tau_{1}-\tau_{2})+G_{+-}(q,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\Theta(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1}).

(177)

The analytic continuation of
G_{+-}
is straightforward, since
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
are continued according to their labels,
\tau_{1}=\mathrm{i}z_{1}
and
\tau_{2}=-\mathrm{i}z_{2}
. Using (
159
), we have

\displaystyle G_{+-}(q,\mathrm{i}z_{1},-\mathrm{i}z_{2})

\displaystyle=G_{-+}(q,-\mathrm{i}z_{1},\mathrm{i}z_{2})=a_{\beta}q^{-2\beta}\mathcal{K}_{\beta}(q,z_{1})\mathcal{K}_{\beta}(q,z_{2}).

(178)

Note that these propagators are real. Furthermore, the factor of
q^{-2\beta}
has this form for all
\beta>0
and no logarithms are present.

For the analytic continuation of
G_{++}
and
G_{--}
we need more relations. This happens because for
G_{++}
we continue both times in the same direction,
\tau_{1}=\mathrm{i}z_{1}
and
\tau_{2}=\mathrm{i}z_{2}
. This means that we have
H_{\beta}^{(2)}(-\mathrm{i}qz_{1})
, which continues according to (
159
), but also
H_{\beta}^{(1)}(-\mathrm{i}qz_{2})
, which now has the incorrect sign. Instead, for
x>0
we use the identities

\displaystyle H_{\beta}^{(1)}(-\mathrm{i}x)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\mathrm{i}}{\pi}e^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi\beta}{2}}K_{\beta}(e^{-\mathrm{i}\pi}x),

\displaystyle H_{\beta}^{(2)}(\mathrm{i}x)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\mathrm{i}}{\pi}e^{\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi\beta}{2}}K_{\beta}(e^{\mathrm{i}\pi}x),

(179)

which then can be composed with

\displaystyle K_{\beta}(e^{-\mathrm{i}\pi}x)

\displaystyle=e^{\mathrm{i}\pi\beta}K_{\beta}(x)+\mathrm{i}\pi I_{\beta}(x),

\displaystyle K_{\beta}(e^{\mathrm{i}\pi}x)

\displaystyle=e^{-\mathrm{i}\pi\beta}K_{\beta}(x)-\mathrm{i}\pi I_{\beta}(x)

(180)

to obtain

\displaystyle H_{\beta}^{(1)}(-\mathrm{i}x)

\displaystyle=2e^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi\beta}{2}}I_{\beta}(x)-\frac{2\mathrm{i}}{\pi}e^{\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi\beta}{2}}K_{\beta}(x),

\displaystyle H_{\beta}^{(2)}(\mathrm{i}x)

\displaystyle=2e^{\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi\beta}{2}}I_{\beta}(x)+\frac{2\mathrm{i}}{\pi}e^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi\beta}{2}}K_{\beta}(x).

(181)

This is how we will now get Bessel-
I
’s into the game.

We also have to deal with the Heaviside thetas. We deform the contour as presented in figure
3
. Since the integrand is continuous, the contributions from both arcs cancel and we have to substitute

\displaystyle\Theta(\tau_{1}-\tau_{2})\longmapsto\Theta(z_{2}-z_{1}).

(182)

To write the analytic continuations of the propagators we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\beta}(q,z)

\displaystyle=2^{\beta-1}\Gamma(\beta)q^{-\beta}z^{\frac{d}{2}}I_{\beta}(qz).

(183)

We find

\displaystyle G_{+-}(q,\mathrm{i}z_{1},\mathrm{i}z_{2})

\displaystyle=e^{\mathrm{i}\pi(\beta-\frac{d}{2})}q^{-2\beta}a_{\beta}\mathcal{K}_{\beta}(q,z_{1})\mathcal{K}_{\beta}(q,z_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\mathrm{i}e^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi d}{2}}\mathcal{K}_{\beta}(q,z_{1})\mathcal{I}_{\beta}(q,z_{2})

(184)

and

\displaystyle G_{+-}(q,\mathrm{i}z_{1},\mathrm{i}z_{2})=\left[G_{-+}(q,-\mathrm{i}z_{1},-\mathrm{i}z_{2})\right]^{\ast}=G_{-+}(q,\mathrm{i}z_{2},\mathrm{i}z_{1})=\left[G_{+-}(q,-\mathrm{i}z_{2},-\mathrm{i}z_{1})\right]^{\ast}.

(185)

This leads to

\displaystyle G_{\pm\pm}(q,\pm\mathrm{i}z_{1},\pm\mathrm{i}z_{2})

\displaystyle=e^{\pm\mathrm{i}\pi(\beta-\frac{d}{2})}q^{-2\beta}a_{\beta}\mathcal{K}_{\beta}(q,z_{1})\mathcal{K}_{\beta}(q,z_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\pm\mathrm{i}e^{\mp\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi d}{2}}\mathcal{G}_{\beta}(q,z_{1},z_{2}),

(186)

where
\mathcal{G}_{\beta}
denotes the AdS bulk-to-bulk propagator,

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\beta}(q,z_{1},z_{2})=\mathcal{K}_{\beta}(q,z_{1})\mathcal{I}_{\beta}(q,z_{2})\Theta(z_{1}-z_{2})+\mathcal{K}_{\beta}(q,z_{2})\mathcal{I}_{\beta}(q,z_{1})\Theta(z_{2}-z_{1}).

(187)

With all these analytic continuations we can finally evaluate (
174
) and (
175
). We obtain the following amplitudes

\displaystyle I_{++}

\displaystyle=-e^{\mathrm{i}\pi d}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}z_{1}}{z_{1}^{d+1}}G_{+}(q_{1},\mathrm{i}z_{1})G_{+}(q_{2},\mathrm{i}z_{1})\times

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}z_{2}}{z_{2}^{d+1}}G_{++}(s,\mathrm{i}z_{1},\mathrm{i}z_{2})G_{+}(q_{3},\mathrm{i}z_{2})G_{+}(q_{4},\mathrm{i}z_{2})

\displaystyle=-(-\tau_{0})^{2d-\beta_{T}}e^{\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi}{2}(\beta_{T}-d)}\prod_{j=1}^{4}a_{\beta_{j}}q_{j}^{-2\beta_{j}}\times\left[\mathrm{i}\times i^{\text{fin}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}+\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.+e^{\mathrm{i}\pi\beta_{x}}a_{\beta_{x}}s^{-2\beta_{x}}i^{\text{fin}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{x}]}(q_{1},q_{2},s)i^{\text{fin}}_{[\Delta_{x}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}(s,q_{3},q_{4})\right],

(188)

\displaystyle I_{+-}

\displaystyle=(-\tau_{0})^{2d-\beta_{T}}e^{\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi}{2}(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}-\beta_{3}-\beta_{3})}\prod_{j=1}^{4}a_{\beta_{j}}q_{j}^{-2\beta_{j}}\times

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times a_{\beta_{x}}s^{-2\beta_{x}}i^{\text{fin}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{x}]}(q_{1},q_{2},s)i^{\text{fin}}_{[\Delta_{x}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}(s,q_{3},q_{4}),

(189)

where
i_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}
denotes the AdS 4-point exchange amplitude as defined in
Bzowski:2022rlz
. All in all the 4-point function amplitude reads

\displaystyle ds_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}=2\lim_{\tau_{0}\rightarrow 0^{-}}(-\tau_{0})^{\Delta_{T}-4d}\re\left[I_{++}+I_{+-}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=2\prod_{j=1}^{4}a_{\beta_{j}}q_{j}^{-2\beta_{j}}\left[i^{\text{fin}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}\times\sin\left(\frac{\pi}{2}(\beta_{T}-d)\right)\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+2a_{\beta_{x}}s^{-2\beta_{x}}i^{\text{fin}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{x}]}(q_{1},q_{2},s)i^{\text{fin}}_{[\Delta_{x}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}(s,q_{3},q_{4})\times

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.\times\sin\left(\frac{\pi}{2}(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\beta_{x}-\frac{d}{2})\right)\sin\left(\frac{\pi}{2}(\beta_{x}+\beta_{3}+\beta_{4}-\frac{d}{2})\right)\right].

(190)

The factors under the sines work out correctly in such a way that, for finite or regulated amplitudes one can substitute

\displaystyle\sin\left[\frac{\pi}{2}(\beta_{T}-d)\right]i^{\text{reg}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3},q_{4},s)

\displaystyle=\im i^{\text{reg}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}(\mathrm{i}q_{1},\mathrm{i}q_{2},\mathrm{i}q_{3},\mathrm{i}q_{4},\mathrm{i}s),

(191)

\displaystyle\sin\left(\frac{\pi}{2}(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\beta_{x}-\frac{d}{2})\right)i^{\text{reg}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{x}]}(q_{1},q_{2},s)

\displaystyle=\im i^{\text{reg}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{x}]}(\mathrm{i}q_{1},\mathrm{i}q_{2},\mathrm{i}s).

(192)

Finally, we can rewrite the factors of
q_{j}^{-2\beta_{j}}
and
s^{-2\beta_{j}}
using (
165
). In this way one finds

\displaystyle ds^{\text{reg}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}\prod_{j=1}^{4}\frac{1}{\im i^{\text{reg}}_{[\Delta_{j}\Delta_{j}]}(\mathrm{i}q_{j})}\left[\im i^{\text{reg}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}(\mathrm{i}q_{j},\mathrm{i}s)\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\left.-\,\frac{\im i^{\text{reg}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{x}]}(\mathrm{i}q_{1},\mathrm{i}q_{2},\mathrm{i}s)\im i^{\text{reg}}_{[\Delta_{x}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}(\mathrm{i}s,\mathrm{i}q_{3},\mathrm{i}q_{4})}{\im i^{\text{reg}}_{[\Delta_{x}\Delta_{x}]}(\mathrm{i}s)}\right].

(193)

4
Regularisation and renormalisation

4.1
Outline of renormalisation

d
0
\Delta_{j}
m_{j}^{2}L^{2}
AdS
dS
\dfrac{d^{2}}{4}
-\dfrac{d^{2}}{4}
\dfrac{d}{2}-1
\dfrac{d}{2}
AdS BF bound
dS BF bound

Figure 6
:
The squared mass as a function of the operator dimension
\Delta_{j}
in AdS (blue) and dS (red). The shaded region indicates the unitarity bound
d/2-1\leq\Delta_{j}\leq d
.

In the previous section we expressed 2-, 3-, and 4-point regulated de Sitter amplitudes in terms of regulated AdS amplitudes. In general some of the presented expressions are divergent. In the cosmology literature the spacetime dimension
d
and the masses
m_{j}^{2}
(equivalently the dimensions
\Delta_{j}
) are usually considered as fixed parameters and the de Sitter correlation functions are calculated with a cut-off
\tau_{0}
on the time coordinate
\tau\leq\tau_{0}<0
, see for example
Falk:1992sf
;
Zaldarriaga:2003my
;
Seery:2008qj
;
Creminelli:2011mw
. Recall that we are considering here light scalars,
0\leq m_{j}^{2}\leq d^{2}/4L_{dS}^{2}
. The upper limit in the this inequality is the de Sitter analogue

Skenderis:2006fb
of the Breitenlohner-Freedman (BF) bound
Breitenlohner:1982bm
;
Breitenlohner:1982jf
. With masses in this range, the dual operators are relevant or marginal with dimension above the unitarity bound,
d/2-1\leq\Delta_{j}\leq d

10
10

10

The range of dimensions
d/2-1\leq\Delta_{j}<d/2
(realised in the mass range
d^{2}/4-1\leq m^{2}L_{dS}^{2}<d^{2}/4
) is special, as discussed in the AdS context in
Klebanov:1999tb
. In this paper we restrict ourselves to
\Delta_{j}>d/2
.
.
Irrelevant operators
\Delta_{j}>d
are mapped to negative masses in dS, while massive fields with positive mass outside the above range correspond to tachyonic fields with mass below the BF bound in AdS. Such fields correspond to operators with complex dimensions and the systematic discussion of such cases from the perspective of holography is still an open question, see
Isono:2020qew
for work in this direction.

When
d/2<\Delta_{j}<d
the asymptotic expansion of the de Sitter bulk field
\varphi
has the form (
53
), with
\varphi_{(0)}
being the most leading coefficient. When
\Delta_{j}=d/2
, the solution of the free equation of motion
\varphi_{\{0\}}
(see (
39
)-(
2.1
))
already contains logarithms, see
Bianchi:2001de
;
Bianchi:2001kw
,
and similarly when
\Delta_{j}=d
the perturbative solution to the Klein-Gordon equation (
62
) contains more leading, logarithmic terms, and the perturbative solution,
\varphi_{\{k\}}
, of order
k
may contain
\log^{k}(-\tau)
terms, see
Bzowski:2015pba
. In order to define the limit
\tau_{0}\to 0
and, consequently, the boundary correlation functions, one must renormalise the theory. This can be done similarly to holographic renormalisation in AdS/CFT, with suitable counterterms added at a cut-off surface at
\tau_{0}<0
. Then, the limit
\tau_{0}\rightarrow 0
can be taken.

Let us recall that holographic renormalisation in AdS involves both the addition of local counterterms and renormalisation of the sources. While the addition of counterterms was considered since the early days of AdS/CFT
Henningson:1998gx
;
Henningson:1998ey
, the need to renormalise the sources was realised much later
Bzowski:2015pba
11
11

11

One reason for this is that such renormalisation is needed only for 3-point functions and higher.
and is less well-known. Let us briefly summarise how this works. Let
\varphi^{i}_{(0)}(\bm{x})
be the fields parametrising the boundary conditions of the bulk fields
\varphi^{i}(z,\bm{x})
, as in (
29
), where
i
is an index enumerating bulks field (in general of different mass and interactions). Then to derive renormalised correlators we need a counterterm action
S_{\rm ct}[\varphi_{(0)}^{i};\varepsilon]
, which is local in
\varphi^{i}_{(0)}
, where
\varepsilon
is (any) regulator, and in addition we need to renormalise the sources, schematically as

\varphi^{i}_{(0)}=\varphi^{i}_{(0)}[\phi_{(0)}^{j},\varepsilon]=\phi_{(0)}^{i}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Box^{k_{1}}\phi_{(0)}^{j_{1}}\Box^{k_{2}}\phi_{(0)}^{j_{2}}+\cdots

(194)

where
k_{1},k_{2}
are integers and the derivatives are along the boundary directions (in general all possible contractions of the derivatives must considered). In this schematic expression the pole is a placeholder for singularity – the singularity may also logarithmic.
Such renormalisation is possible only when the spectrum of the theory contains operators of suitable dimension such that the second term on the right-hand side of (
194
) has the same scaling dimension as the first one. This is precisely one of the conditions for the appearance of short-distance singularities in 3-point functions
Bzowski:2013sza
;
Bzowski:2015pba
. The dots indicate additional contributions that may be needed to renormalise short-distance singularities in higher-point functions. Then renormalised correlators are obtained by functionally differentiating with respect to
\phi^{i}_{(0)}
the renormalised on-shell action,

S_{\rm ren}[\phi^{i}_{(0)}]=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left(S_{\rm on-shell}[\varphi^{i}_{(0)}[\phi_{(0)}^{j},\varepsilon];\varepsilon]+S_{\rm ct}[\varphi^{i}_{(0)}[\phi_{(0)}^{j},\varepsilon];\varepsilon]\right)\,.

(195)

Now recall that in the AdS/CFT correspondence the field parametrising the boundary conditions,
\varphi^{i}_{(0)}
, is considered as the source that couples to the corresponding gauge invariant operator
\mathcal{O}
, namely in the CFT we have the coupling

S_{\rm CFT}[\varphi^{i}_{(0)},\mathcal{O}_{i}]=\int d^{d}x\varphi^{i}_{(0)}\mathcal{O}_{i}\,.

(196)

Then the renormalisation of the sources amounts to additional counterterms on the CFT side that renormalise these couplings of the schematic form,

S^{\rm ct}_{\rm CFT}[\phi^{i}_{(0)},\mathcal{O}_{i};\varepsilon]=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int d^{d}x\Box^{k_{1}}\phi_{(0)}^{j_{1}}\Box^{k_{2}}\phi_{(0)}^{j_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{i}

(197)

As we will see below, the usual AdS counterterms
S_{\rm ct}[\varphi_{(0)}^{i};\varepsilon]
are projected out when we go from AdS to dS but the analogues of (
197
) survive.

In this paper we will not use a time cut-off. Rather, just as in its AdS counterparts
Bzowski:2022rlz
, we will work within dimensional regularisation and renormalisation. This means that we set
\tau_{0}=0

from the beginning
and keep
d
and
\Delta_{j}
as free parameters. As the amplitudes are analytic in
d
and
\Delta_{j}
, one may generically define the amplitudes by starting from values of
d,\Delta_{j}
where the amplitudes are finite and then analytically continue to the desired
d,\Delta_{j}
. Only for specific values of the dimensions amplitudes cannot be defined in this way and require explicit subtractions. This happens on a hyperplane (or, in more special cases, intersections of hyperplanes) in the space spanned by
d
and
\Delta_{j}
.
In such a case boundary counterterms should be added to the effective action to yield the renormalised amplitude finite.

We regulate the theory by shifting the dimensions
d
and
\Delta_{j}
away from the singular point. As in case of AdS calculations, the regulator will be denoted by
\epsilon
and we use the
beta-scheme
where the regulated dimensions
\hat{d}
and
\hat{\Delta}_{j}
are

\displaystyle d\,\longmapsto\,\hat{d}=d+2\epsilon,

\displaystyle\Delta_{j}\,\longmapsto\,\hat{\Delta}_{j}=\Delta_{j}+\epsilon.

(198)

The idea behind the beta-scheme is that the combinations appearing as the orders of the Bessel functions in expressions such as (
43
) and (
66
) remain unregulated,

\displaystyle\beta_{j}=\Delta_{j}-\frac{d}{2}\,\longmapsto\,\hat{\beta}_{j}=\beta_{j}.

(199)

The beta-scheme regulates all correlation functions except for 2-point functions. However, for the case of
d=3
analyzed in this paper, the 2-point functions under consideration are all finite.

In order to renormalise
12
12

12

Note that this renormalisation is about IR divergences at tree-level. We are not addressing UV issues and IR issues at loop level in this paper.
the de Sitter amplitudes computed using (
149
) we want to add a counterterm action at
\tau=0
. The counterterm action
S_{\rm ct}=S_{ct}[\varphi_{(0)},J_{+},J_{-};\mu,\mathfrak{a}_{j};\epsilon]
is a functional of the boundary value
\varphi_{(0)}
of the bulk field and the sources
J_{+},J_{-}
. It also depends on the renormalisation constant
\mu
and a set of renormalisation constants
\mathfrak{a}_{j}
parameterising scheme dependence. It is entirely contained within the boundary at
\tau=0
and it only contributes a boundary term to correlation functions. Two complex conjugate copies of the boundary counterterm must be then included in the Schwinger-Keldysh generating functional (
149
) by replacing

\displaystyle S_{+}[\varphi_{+}]\longmapsto S_{+}[\varphi_{+}]+S_{\rm ct}[\varphi_{(0)},J_{+}],

\displaystyle S_{-}[\varphi_{-}]\longmapsto S_{-}[\varphi_{-}]+S_{\rm ct}[\varphi_{(0)},J_{-}]

(200)

When the contribution from the counterterms is added to the regulated correlation functions, the limit
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
limit should exist.

The counterterm action
S_{\rm ct}
must be real,
S_{\rm ct}=S^{\ast}_{\rm ct}
, just as the original de Sitter action.
Any counterterm that only depends on
\varphi_{(0)}
will cancel between the forward and backward path of the Schwinger-Keldysh path integral. Such terms correspond to the traditional AdS counterterms. Another way to see that the contribution of the traditional counterterms vanishes is to note that they are analytic in momenta and analytically continuing from AdS to dS
and taking the imaginary part projects these terms out. Counterterms that only depend on
\varphi_{(0)}
are responsible for conformal anomalies in AdS
Henningson:1998gx
. The fact that these terms cancel means that there are no conformal anomalies in de Sitter. This point was also made recently in
Chakraborty:2023yed
;
Chakraborty:2023los
.

Now, note that the coupling
J_{+}
and
J_{-}
to
\varphi_{(0)}
at
\tau=0

in (
149
) is the same as the coupling of
\varphi_{(0)}
to
O
in (
196
).
13
13

13

Recall that
\lim_{\tau\to 0^{-}}\varphi_{+}(\tau,\bm{x})=\lim_{\tau\to 0^{-}}\varphi_{-}(\tau,\bm{x})=\varphi_{(0)}(\bm{x})
.
Then the AdS results suggest that we need a counterterm of the form (
197
). Thus we expect that we should be able to renormalise the de Sitter amplitudes using a counterterm of the form,

S_{\rm ct}^{dS}[\varphi_{(0)},(J_{+}-J_{-});\mu,\mathfrak{a}_{j};\epsilon]=\int d^{d}x(J_{+}-J_{-})f\left(\varphi_{(0)};\mu,\mathfrak{a}_{j};\epsilon\right)

(201)

for a suitable local function
f
of
\varphi_{(0)}
(that also depends on the regulator
\epsilon
, a renormalisation scale
\mu
and constants
\mathfrak{a}_{j}
parametrising the scheme dependence). We will indeed see that such counterterm is sufficient to renormalise all cases. In a sense holographic renormalisation in dS is much simpler than in AdS.

Usually to carry out the renormalisation procedure the action of the regulated theory,
S
, must be specified. Then, it is the correlation functions in this theory that get renormalised. Here, however, we will follow
Bzowski:2022rlz
and renormalise tree-level amplitudes instead. As discussed in
Bzowski:2022rlz
, each such amplitude is a conformal correlator in some holographic CFT described by a suitable bulk action.
As far as 3- and 4-point functions are concerned, we will consider the “asymmetric” theories given by the actions (
151
) and (
167
) and apply the renormalisation procedure. When this is achieved, we effectively renormalise the amplitudes
ds_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}
as well as
ds_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}
and
ds_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}
in (
154
) and (
3.5
). As these are obtained in the least symmetric theory, any other 3- and 4-point functions in more symmetric theories can be obtained as linear combinations of these amplitudes. Thus, any renormalised correlation function can then be expressed in terms of the renormalised amplitudes.

4.1.1
Results

The procedure outlined above leads to renormalised de Sitter amplitudes. These objects, denoted by
ds^{\text{ren}}
, depend on the renormaalisation scale
\mu_{dS}
as well as some scheme-dependent renormalisation constants
\mathfrak{a}_{i}^{dS}
, in addition to their momentum dependence,
ds^{\text{ren}}=ds^{\text{ren}}(q_{i};\mu_{dS},\mathfrak{a}^{dS}_{i})
. The main question we want to address in this section is whether the renormalised de Sitter amplitudes,
ds^{\text{ren}}
, can be obtained from renormalised anti-de Sitter amplitudes,
i^{\text{ren}}
, by applying the same analytic continuations as in (
144
), (
166
), (
3.5.1
) and (
3.5.2
). In other words, we want to write

\displaystyle ds^{\text{ren}}_{[\Delta\Delta]}(q)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{\im i^{\text{ren}}_{[\Delta\Delta]}(\mathrm{i}q)},

(202)

\displaystyle ds^{\text{ren}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}(q_{i})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}\frac{\im i^{\text{ren}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}(\mathrm{i}q_{i})}{\prod_{j=1}^{3}\im i^{\text{ren}}_{[\Delta_{j}\Delta_{j}]}(\mathrm{i}q_{j})},

(203)

\displaystyle ds^{\text{ren}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}(q_{i})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{8}\frac{\im i^{\text{ren}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}(\mathrm{i}q_{i})}{\prod_{j=1}^{4}\im i^{\text{ren}}_{[\Delta_{j}\Delta_{j}]}(\mathrm{i}q_{j})},

(204)

\displaystyle ds^{\text{ren}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}(q_{i},s)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}\prod_{j=1}^{4}\frac{1}{\im i^{\text{ren}}_{[\Delta_{j}\Delta_{j}]}(\mathrm{i}q_{j})}\left[\im i^{\text{ren}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}(\mathrm{i}q_{i},\mathrm{i}s)\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\left.-\,\frac{\im i^{\text{ren}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{x}]}(\mathrm{i}q_{1},\mathrm{i}q_{2},\mathrm{i}s)\im i^{\text{ren}}_{[\Delta_{x}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}(\mathrm{i}s,\mathrm{i}q_{3},\mathrm{i}q_{4})}{\im i^{\text{ren}}_{[\Delta_{x}\Delta_{x}]}(\mathrm{i}s)}\right].

(205)

The two sides depend on different sets of renormalisation data. On the left-hand side, the amplitudes depend on the renormalisation scale
\mu_{dS}
and a set of scheme-dependent constants
\mathfrak{a}_{i}^{dS}
. On the right-hand side, the renormalised AdS amplitudes,
i^{\text{ren}}
, depend on the AdS renormalisation scale
\mu_{AdS}
and another set of scheme-dependent constants
\mathfrak{b}_{i}^{AdS}
. Can we equate the variables on both sides so that (
202
)-(
205
) hold?

We can always equate the renormalisation scales

\displaystyle\mu=\mu_{dS}=\mu_{AdS}

(206)

and from now on we use
\mu
only. While the scheme-dependent constants
\mathfrak{a}_{i}^{dS}
and
\mathfrak{b}_{i}^{AdS}
generally do not match, there always exists a choice of the scheme-dependent constants
\mathfrak{b}_{i}^{AdS}
on the AdS side such that the formulae (
202
)-(
205
) hold.
More precisely, there exists polynomial functions which map AdS scheme-dependent constants to dS constants,
\mathfrak{a}^{dS}_{i}=\mathfrak{a}^{dS}_{i}(\mathfrak{b}^{AdS}_{j})
, such that the holographic formulae (
202
)-(
205
) hold. These functions do not depend on the momenta, nor the renormalisation scale, nor any other constants (such as the coupling constants) present in the system. For the precise formulation, see subsection
4.3
.

4.2
Divergences

All de Sitter 2-point amplitudes
ds^{\text{reg}}_{[\Delta\Delta]}
are finite. As discussed earlier, this differs from the AdS case, where the amplitudes
i^{\text{reg}}_{[\Delta\Delta]}
are divergent whenever
\beta=\Delta-\frac{d}{2}
is a non-negative integer. Most of the de Sitter amplitudes are less singular than their AdS counterparts, but not all. The orders of the poles at
\epsilon=0
are listed in the tables below.

The dS 3-point amplitudes under consideration are either finite or exhibit a linear divergence as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
. Their degrees of divergence are listed in the second column of table
1
, while the third column contains the degrees of divergence of the corresponding AdS amplitudes.

exhibits a double pole, while

is finite.

.
